## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:** The champion (score 5571.45) uses GoToColor(blue, B=8) as the initial state - robots move toward blue walls to find the target strip. Then FlockCenter (B=10) aggregates them, with Repulsion (B=5) to avoid overcrowding and Exploration (B=0) as a fallback. The BLACK floor condition (C=0) triggers the transition from GoToColor to FlockCenter, meaning once on the black floor (target strip), robots switch to flocking.

**Champion structure analysis:**
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → S1 if BLACK floor (p=0.8), → S2 if many neighbors (≥8), → S2 with small prob
- S1: FlockCenter → S2 if few neighbors (<5), → S0 if small prob (escape)
- S2: Repulsion → self-loop avoided → S0 if BLACK floor, stays repulsing
- S3: Exploration → S0 if GRAY floor, → S1 if many neighbors

Wait, let me re-read: S2 transitions go to S2 (self-loop not allowed per rules)... Actually --n2x0 2 means destination index 2 among OTHER states. With 4 states leaving state 2, others are [0,1,3], so D=2 → state 3. So S2→S3, and S3→S0, S3→S1.

**Strategy:**
1. Copy champion exactly (survival)
2. Exploit champion: tune probabilities and thresholds
3. Exploit #2 (simple 2-state GoToColor+FlockCenter)
4. Exploit #3 (3-state version)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 3:** The new champion is a simple 2-state machine: GoToColor(blue) → FlockCenter. 
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → S1 if BLACK floor (p=0.9), or → S1 if many neighbors (≥5, steep sigmoid)
- S1: FlockCenter(90) → S0 if few neighbors (<2), or → S0 with tiny prob (0.01)

This is elegant: robots move toward blue walls until they hit the black floor (target strip), then flock together. The high FC gain (90) keeps them tight. The "few neighbors" condition (threshold=2) lets isolated robots go back to seeking.

**What works:** GoToColor(blue/clr=3) + BLACK floor detection as trigger + FlockCenter with high gain.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Tune FC gain (80-100), threshold (1-3), sigmoid steepness, floor probability
3. Add a 3rd state for repulsion when overcrowded
4. Try different neighbor thresholds in S1's return condition
5. Try slightly different probabilities on the floor condition

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor → FlockCenter → Repulsion (when too many neighbors)
- 2-state with attraction instead of FlockCenter
- 4-state version of the champion structure

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 4:** The champion is a 2-state machine: GoToColor(blue) → Attraction. 
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → S1 if BLACK floor (p=0.9), OR → S1 if many neighbors (≥5, w=8)
- S1: Attraction(4) → S0 if few neighbors (<2, w=12), OR → S0 with tiny prob (0.01)

This beats FlockCenter! Attraction toward nearby robots while on the black (target) strip is more effective than FlockCenter. The "few neighbors" trigger sends isolated robots back to seek the blue wall.

**What's working:**
- GoToColor(blue, clr=3) as search state
- BLACK floor (C=0) as the "arrived at target" trigger with high probability (0.9)
- High neighbor count trigger (C=3, p=5, w=8) also sends to aggregation
- Attraction (B=4) with gain=4 for aggregation
- Few-neighbors (C=4, p=2, w=12) sends back to search

**Exploitation:**
- Tune att gain (3.0-5.0), neighbor thresholds, sigmoid steepness
- Try FlockCenter variant with similar structure
- Add 3rd state for repulsion when overcrowded

**Exploration:**
- Try att gain = 5.0 (max)
- Try different neighbor count thresholds for escape
- 3-state with repulsion buffer

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 5:** The champion is a clean 2-state machine:
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → S1 if BLACK floor (p=0.9), OR → S1 if ≥5 neighbors (w=8)
- S1: Attraction(4) → S0 if <3 neighbors (w=10), OR → S0 with tiny prob (0.02)

The top 5 performers are ALL 2-state GoToColor+Attraction variants. The critical parameters seem to be:
- att gain: 4-5 works, 4 slightly better
- neighbor threshold for escape from S1: 3 with w=10 beats 2 with w=12
- neighbor trigger in S0: threshold=5-6, steepness=6-10
- floor probability: 0.9 consistently good

**What to exploit:**
- Fine-tune att gain around 4 (3.5, 4.0, 4.2, 4.5)
- Fine-tune escape threshold (2-4) and steepness (8-15)
- Fine-tune S0 neighbor trigger (threshold 4-6, steepness 6-12)
- Vary the random escape probability (0.01-0.03)

**What to explore:**
- 3-state: add a "stay still" state once fully aggregated (Stop when many neighbors)
- Try FlockCenter with the winning S0 structure but tuned fc
- Try adding a 3rd transition in S0 (random small prob escape)

## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 6:** The champion uses escape threshold p=4 (w=10) which beats the previous p=3 (w=10). All top performers are 2-state GoToColor(blue)+Attraction variants. The structure is converging strongly.

**Parameter analysis of top performers:**
- S0: floor=BLACK (p=0.9), neighbor≥5 (w=8-10) → S1
- S1: Attraction(4), escape when <4 neighbors (w=10), tiny random escape (0.015-0.02)
- att gain: 4 consistently best (5 also good)
- Escape threshold: 4 (w=10) now beats 3 (w=10) or 2 (w=12)

**Exploitation focus:**
- Try threshold=4, vary w (8, 10, 12, 15)
- Try threshold=5 with various w
- Try att=4 vs att=4.5 vs att=5 with the winning escape params
- Try p0x0=0.85-0.95 for floor trigger
- Try adding small random escape from S0

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → Stop (when very many neighbors)
- Try w0x1 variations (6, 7, 8, 9) for neighbor trigger in S0
- Try escape prob 0.01-0.025

## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 7:** The champion uses p0x1=6 (neighbor threshold ≥6 in S0) with w=8, which beat p0x1=5. The escape threshold in S1 is p=4 (w=10). All top 5 are 2-state variants.

**Parameter convergence analysis:**
- S0 neighbor trigger: threshold=6, w=8 is NOW best (was 5 before)
- S1 escape: threshold=4, w=10 is consistently best
- att gain: 4 best, 5 also good
- floor prob: 0.9 best (0.92 worse)
- Random escape: 0.02 consistently good
- 3-state machines are worse - stay with 2-state

**Fine-tuning directions:**
- Try threshold=7 in S0 (going higher)
- Try w=9, 10 for S0 neighbor trigger (steeper)
- Try att=4 vs 4.2 vs 4.5 with the new winning escape params
- Try threshold=4 w=8 or w=12 in S1
- Try threshold=3 vs 5 in S1 (escape sensitivity)
- Try floor prob 0.88, 0.9 variations
- Try p0x1=6 with w=7 or w=9

**Exploration:**
- Try a version where S0 has only the floor condition (no neighbor trigger)
- Try threshold=6 w=10 or w=6 in S0

## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 8:** The champion held at 2656.74. The simplest version (1 transition in S0: only floor condition) scored 2661.95 - very close! The neighbor trigger in S0 may not help much. Top performers cluster tightly around the champion structure.

**What we've learned:**
- S0 neighbor trigger: p=6, w=8 is good but maybe not essential (1-transition version nearly ties)
- S1 escape threshold: p=4 or p=5, w=10 both work; p=5 (2702) and p=4 (champion) are close
- att gain: 4 best, 5 also good (2737)
- Random escape: 0.02 best, 0.025 slightly worse, 0.015 slightly worse
- w=10 for escape is best (w=12 worse)
- The scores are very tight - we're near a local optimum

**Fine-tuning for rounds 9:**
- Try S0 with only floor condition (p=0.85, 0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.95)
- Try att=4 with p=4 vs p=5 escape, various w
- Try p0x1=7 or p0x1=8 in S0 (higher neighbor threshold)
- Try w0x1=7 for S0 trigger
- Try random escape 0.018, 0.022
- Try 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → Stop (stay put once very aggregated)

**Exploration:** Try GoAwayColor(green) as an alternative search strategy (move away from green walls to reach blue end).

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 18 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.54 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
